event-type="pmc-release"><date><day>01</day><month>03</month><year>2021</year></date></event><event event-type="pmc-live"><date><day>01</day><month>03</month><year>2021</year></date></event><event event-type="pmc-released-under-embargo"><date><day>27</day><month>02</month><year>2021</year></date></event><event event-type="pmc-last-change"><date iso-8601-date="2024-08-06 09:25:19.400"><day>06</day><month>08</month><year>2024</year></date></event></pub-history><permissions><license><license-p>This file is available for text mining. It may also be used consistent with the principles of fair use under the copyright law.</license-p></license></permissions><self-uri xmlns:xlink="http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink" content-type="pmc-pdf" xlink:href="nihms-1667049.pdf"/><abstract id="ABS1"><p id="P2">Reports of environmental and human health impacts of per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) have greatly increased in the peer-reviewed literature. The goals of the present review are to assess the state of the science regarding toxicological effects of PFAS and to develop strategies for advancing knowledge on the health effects of this large family of chemicals. Currently, much of the toxicity data available for PFAS are for a handful of chemicals, primarily legacy PFAS such as perfluorooctanoic acid and perfluorooctane sulfonate. Epidemiological studies have revealed associations between exposure to specific PFAS and a variety of health effects, including altered immune and thyroid function, liver disease, lipid and insulin dysregulation, kidney disease, adverse reproductive and developmental outcomes, and cancer. Concordance with experimental animal data exists for many of these effects. However, information on modes of action and adverse outcome pathways must be expanded, and profound differences in PFAS toxicokinetic properties must be considered in understanding differences in responses between the sexes and among species and life stages. With many health effects noted for a relatively few example compounds and hundreds of other PFAS in commerce lacking toxicity data, more contemporary and high-throughput approaches such as read-across, molecular dynamics, and protein modeling are proposed to accelerate the development of toxicity information on emerging and legacy PFAS, individually and as mixtures. In addition, an appropriate degree of precaution, given what is already known from the PFAS examples noted, may be needed to protect human health.</p></abstract><kwd-group><kwd>Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances</kwd><kwd>Perfluorooctane sulfonate</kwd><kwd>Perfluorooctanoic acid</kwd><kwd>Persistent compounds</kwd><kwd>Contaminants of emerging concern</kwd></kwd-group><custom-meta-group><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-qastatus</meta-name><meta-value>0</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-live</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-embargo</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-status-released</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-open-access</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-olf</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-manuscript</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-open-manuscript</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-legally-suppressed</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-has-pdf</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-has-supplement</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-pdf-only</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-suppress-copyright</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-is-real-version</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-is-scanned-article</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-preprint</meta-name><meta-value>no</meta-value></custom-meta><custom-meta><meta-name>pmc-prop-in-epmc</meta-name><meta-value>yes</meta-value></custom-meta></custom-meta-group></article-meta></front><body><sec id="S1"><title>INTRODUCTION</title><p id="P3">Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances (PFAS) are ubiquitous in environmental media because of their prolific use in a variety of industrial and consumer products and processes (<xref rid="R129" ref-type="bibr">Jian et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R261" ref-type="bibr">Sunderland et al. 2019</xref>). Widespread human exposure to PFAS in water, food, and air coupled with the lengthy environmental persistence and biological half-lives of some PFAS have led to measurable PFAS in the blood of nearly the entire population in developed countries, with health effects reported globally (<xref rid="R139" ref-type="bibr">Kato et al. 2011</xref>; <xref rid="R140" ref-type="bibr">Khalil et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R259" ref-type="bibr">Stubleski et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R129" ref-type="bibr">Jian et al. 2018</xref>). Information needed to evaluate the potential risk of harm from PFAS includes the types of adverse health effects that might occur at environmentally relevant exposures, especially in sensitive life stages. Information is also needed regarding the mode(s) of action for PFAS toxicity, PFAS toxicokinetics in both humans and laboratory animal models, and dose-response relationships. Risk estimates can be used to inform public health exposure limits that will determine the need for exposure mitigation and environmental cleanup.</p><p id="P4">There are several challenges in obtaining the information needed to assess human health risk from the large number of PFAS with a wide range of structures and chemical properties (<xref rid="R33" ref-type="bibr">Buck et al. 2011</xref>; <xref rid="R288" ref-type="bibr">Wang Z et al. 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R200" ref-type="bibr">Organisation for Economic Co-operation Development 2018</xref>). Data on the identity, composition, and quantity of PFAS used in products and processes are often treated as confidential business information, hampering efforts to estimate exposure sources and routes. The Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development’s (OECD’s) chemical inventory reports over 4000 substances that contain at least one perfluoroalkyl (–CnF2n–) moiety (<xref rid="R200" ref-type="bibr">Organisation for Economic Co-operation Development 2018</xref>), and the US Environmental Protection Agency (USEPA) has a curated list of over 8000 PFAS included, based on structure (<xref rid="R273" ref-type="bibr">US Environmental Protection Agency 2018</xref>) from the CompTox Chemicals Dashboard (<xref rid="R302" ref-type="bibr">Williams et al. 2017</xref>). The USEPA estimates that more than 600 PFAS are currently in commercial use (<xref rid="R274" ref-type="bibr">US Environmental Protection Agency 2019</xref>). Experimental studies of PFAS have been limited by funding and the availability of analytical standards, confounded by the prevalence of background contamination in laboratory materials, and challenged by physicochemical properties such as high surface activity that can interfere with and complicate measurements. Consequently, sufficient information to conduct quantitative risk assessment is currently available for only a relative few PFAS (<xref rid="R209" ref-type="bibr">Post 2020</xref>). Further, although typical human exposures involve various combinations of PFAS (<xref rid="R43" ref-type="bibr">Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2017</xref>), only a few efforts address interactions of PFAS mixtures; and a well-founded, scientific basis on which to evaluate their combined toxic potential does not yet exist (<xref rid="R40" ref-type="bibr">Carr et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R305" ref-type="bibr">Wolf et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R328" ref-type="bibr">Zhou et al. 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R111" ref-type="bibr">Hoover et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R275" ref-type="bibr">US Environmental Protection Agency 2020</xref>).</p><p id="P5">The Society of Environmental Toxicology and Chemistry (SETAC) North America held the focused topic meeting and workshop “Environmental Risk Assessment of PFAS” on 12 to 15 August 2019, covering a wide range of topics related to the characterization of health risks posed by PFAS. The overarching purpose of the meeting was to begin a scientific discussion on how best to approach studying, grouping, and regulating the large number of PFAS to which people and other species are potentially exposed (for charge questions and other details, see <xref rid="R132" ref-type="bibr">Johnson et al. 2020</xref>). We refer to these PFAS as “legacy” (those perfluoroalkyl acids for which there are accumulating health data but that may be phased out or decreased in use) and “emerging” (those which are being used as replacements, often with minimal health effects data). The objectives of the Human Health Toxicity section were to provide an assessment of the state of the science in understanding toxicological effects of PFAS and to explore and discuss strategies for advancing knowledge on the toxicity of individual and groups of PFAS.</p></sec><sec id="S2"><title>CURRENT KNOWLEDGE OF PFAS TOXICITY IN HUMANS</title><p id="P6">Like other chemicals, PFAS are potentially capable of producing a wide range of adverse health effects depending on the circumstances of exposure (magnitude, duration, and route of exposures, etc.) and factors associated with the individuals exposed (e.g., age, sex, ethnicity, health status, and genetic predisposition). Aspects to consider when establishing the health effects of greatest concern are 1) effects for which evidence is the strongest (strength of evidence can come from consistency of effect across studies, strength of effect associations in epidemiological studies, and species concordance, as examples), and 2) effects for which potential impact is greatest (factors contributing to impact can include severity of effect, functional impairment, persistence, and specific age groups that are susceptible, as examples). Brief summaries of candidate PFAS health effects from human and experimental reports are provided in this section (<xref rid="F1" ref-type="fig">Figure 1</xref>).</p><sec id="S3"><title>Immune function</title><p id="P7">Epidemiological studies have explored relationships between PFAS exposure and laboratory biomarkers of immunomodulation, such as vaccine responses. A doubling of perfluorooctane sulfonate (PFOS) in maternal serum was associated with a 39% (<italic toggle="yes">p</italic> &lt; 0.001) reduction in diphtheria antibody concentration in children (age 5 yr), with increased odds of falling below clinically protective values against diphtheria and tetanus at age 7 yr. The authors noted that a “2-fold greater concentration of major PFCs [perfluorinated compounds] in child serum was associated with a difference of −49% (95% CI, −67% to −23%) in the overall antibody concentration” (<xref rid="R100" ref-type="bibr">Grandjean et al. 2012</xref>). Decreased immunological response persisted at age 13 yr (<xref rid="R101" ref-type="bibr">Grandjean et al. 2017</xref>). Adverse associations were also noted for responses to rubella, mumps, and <italic toggle="yes">Hemophilus</italic> influenza vaccinations in children and to vaccinations in adults (<xref rid="R102" ref-type="bibr">Granum et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R167" ref-type="bibr">Looker et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R257" ref-type="bibr">Stein et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R1" ref-type="bibr">Abraham et al. 2020</xref>). In a single study, modest down-regulation of C-reactive protein response, a marker of human systemic inflammation, was also reported to be associated with perfluorooctanoic acid (PFOA) blood levels (<xref rid="R93" ref-type="bibr">Genser et al. 2015</xref>).</p><p id="P8">Disease outcomes linked with immunosuppression such as clinician-recorded diagnoses of childhood infections have also been associated with prenatal exposures to PFOS and perfluorohexane sulfonate (PFHxS) (<xref rid="R97" ref-type="bibr">Goudarzi et al. 2017</xref>). A pregnancy cohort study prospectively detected increased risk of airway and throat infections and diarrhea in children through age 10 yr, correlated with cord-blood PFAS measurements (<xref rid="R119" ref-type="bibr">Impinen et al. 2018</xref>, <xref rid="R118" ref-type="bibr">2019</xref>). A recent review concluded that exposure to PFAS in infancy and childhood resulted in an immunosuppressive effect characterized by an increased incidence of atopic dermatitis and lower respiratory tract infections (<xref rid="R149" ref-type="bibr">Kvalem et al. 2020</xref>). Some of the immunological effects were sex-specific, but the authors cautioned that there were inconsistencies across studies (<xref rid="R149" ref-type="bibr">Kvalem et al. 2020</xref>). Overall, available data provide strong evidence that PFAS exposure can suppress the human immune response.</p><p id="P9">Population studies of immune hyperreactive diseases have resulted in mixed findings. Studies on childhood allergy and asthma outcomes have shown no association with PFAS (<xref rid="R119" ref-type="bibr">Impinen et al. 2018</xref>, <xref rid="R118" ref-type="bibr">2019</xref>), whereas others have found substantial effects, including provocative evidence that subgroups of individuals not adequately immunized may be at an increased risk for disease a priori (<xref rid="R214" ref-type="bibr">Qin et al. 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R269" ref-type="bibr">Timmermann et al. 2017a</xref>). For example, a case-control study of Taiwanese children compared the first and fourth quartiles of serum measurements for 11 PFAS with asthma and other immune markers and reported confidence intervals well above 1.0 for PFOA and others (<xref rid="R214" ref-type="bibr">Qin et al. 2017</xref>). However, review articles concerning PFAS and childhood allergy and asthma offer nuanced, age- and sex-specific interpretations and advise against firm conclusions (<xref rid="R149" ref-type="bibr">Kvalem et al. 2020</xref>).</p><p id="P10">Chronic autoimmune outcomes, including thyroid disease (see section <xref rid="S4" ref-type="sec">Thyroid function</xref>) and inflammatory bowel disease (IBD), have also been considered. A study in contaminated communities (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 32 254) detected an association between both prevalence and incidence of ulcerative colitis (UC) and PFOA exposure (linear trend <italic toggle="yes">p</italic> = 0.0001 [<xref rid="R256" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2013</xref>]). A worker study (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 3713) found a higher prevalence (<italic toggle="yes">p</italic> = 0.01) and incidence (<italic toggle="yes">p</italic> &lt; 0.05) of UC with increasing log PFOA serum concentrations (<xref rid="R255" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2015</xref>). A case-control study of children and young adults from a background exposure community in Atlanta, Georgia, USA, also found higher serum PFOA levels in patients with UC (<xref rid="R251" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2018b</xref>). In contrast to PFOA-related associations in US populations, a study of a contaminated community in Sweden (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 63 074) did not show a consistent association of IBD with any PFAS exposure (<xref rid="R313" ref-type="bibr">Xu et al. 2020b</xref>).</p><p id="P11">Recent, thorough reviews (<xref rid="R185" ref-type="bibr">National Toxicology Program 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R63" ref-type="bibr">DeWitt et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R202" ref-type="bibr">Pachkowski et al. 2019</xref>) emphasize some key concepts: 1) there is concordance between animal studies and human epidemiological observations that PFAS modify the immune response, and 2) there are noted complexities in assuming dose-response continuums, including possible differences in life-stage vulnerability. Authors of these reviews note uncertainty about which outcome will be of most importance but agree that immunotoxicity should be included among sensitive human PFAS toxicity endpoints.</p></sec><sec id="S4"><title>Thyroid function</title><p id="P12">The C8 Science Panelists concluded that there is a “probable link” of PFOA exposure to thyroid disease, with sex-specific outcomes in women (for hyperthyroid disease) versus men (hypothyroid disease) (<xref rid="R37" ref-type="bibr">C8 Science Panel 2012</xref>). Subsequent reviews drew attention to hypothyroid outcomes in women and children and to the possibility that populations with a priori circulating antithyroid peroxidase antibodies may be at additional risk (<xref rid="R55" ref-type="bibr">Coperchini et al. 2017</xref>). A broad childhood disease review noted “some evidence” that PFAS cause childhood hypothyroidism and characterized the number of studies as “limited” for childhood disease conclusions (<xref rid="R219" ref-type="bibr">Rappazzo et al. 2017</xref>). A meta-analysis of 12 child and adult studies that excluded populations with higher exposures noted that PFAS exposure is negatively associated with serum total thyroxine levels and that “PFAS could induce thyroid dysfunction and disease” (<xref rid="R154" ref-type="bibr">Lee and Choi 2017</xref>).</p><p id="P13">Human thyroid disease is mostly the result of an autoimmune response and is 5 to 10 times more prevalent in women than men (<xref rid="R264" ref-type="bibr">Tadic et al. 2018</xref>). Concerning PFAS and clinically diagnosed outcomes, women in the highest quartile of PFOA exposure (&gt;5.7 ng/mL) reported clinical hypothyroid disease (odds ratio 2.2, 95% confidence interval [CI] 1.4–3.7) over 3 cycles of National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) data (1999–2006, <italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 3974 adults), with similar findings in men (<xref rid="R182" ref-type="bibr">Melzer et al. 2010</xref>). The C8 Science Panel studies (median serum PFOA 26.1 ng/mL) found thyroid disease hazard ratios of 1.00, 1.24, 1.27, 1.36, and 1.37 across cumulative exposure quintiles in women (log-linear trend <italic toggle="yes">p</italic> = 0.03 [<xref rid="R304" ref-type="bibr">Winquist and Steenland 2014b</xref>]), with parallel hypothyroid findings in children aged 1 to 17 yr (<xref rid="R168" ref-type="bibr">Lopez-Espinosa et al. 2012</xref>). The Ronneby, Sweden, population experienced excess risk of thyroid disease in a discrete time period (1984–2005) among women (hazard ratio 1.29, 95% CI 1.05–1.57) that did not persist over time despite higher cumulative PFAS exposure (<xref rid="R6" ref-type="bibr">Andersson et al. 2019</xref>). The authors did not link exposure to hypothyroid outcome, noting a nonmonotonic dose-response relationship (<xref rid="R6" ref-type="bibr">Andersson et al. 2019</xref>).</p><p id="P14">Human population studies augment experimental data that PFAS interact with thyroid hormone binding proteins (<xref rid="R23" ref-type="bibr">Berg et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R222" ref-type="bibr">Ren et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R322" ref-type="bibr">Zhang J et al. 2016</xref>), one of several mechanisms by which PFAS can perturb feedback relationships between free thyroid hormone and the hypothalamic-pituitary-thyroid axis. Exposures to PFAS also interfere with thyroid peroxidase (TPO) enzyme activity in vitro (<xref rid="R245" ref-type="bibr">Song et al. 2012</xref>). Several PFAS studies have pursued this putative mechanism, finding that maternal and neonatal thyroid hormone outcomes were more readily detected in those with a priori abnormally high circulating anti-TPO antibodies (<xref rid="R293" ref-type="bibr">Webster et al. 2014</xref>, <xref rid="R292" ref-type="bibr">2016</xref>). One case-control study investigated congenital hypothyroidism, a rare condition. Serum concentrations of PFOA (5.40 vs 2.12 ng/mL; <italic toggle="yes">p</italic> &lt; 0.01), perfluorononanoic acid (PFNA; 1.93 vs 0.63 ng/mL; <italic toggle="yes">p</italic> &lt; 0.001), perfluorodecanoic acid (PFDA; 0.52 vs 0.30 ng/mL; <italic toggle="yes">p</italic> &lt; 0.005), and perfluoroundecanoic acid (0.98 vs 0.44 ng/mL; <italic toggle="yes">p</italic> &lt; 0.005) were higher in the diagnosed newborns; and levels of several PFAS, including PFOA and PFHxS, were correlated with thyroid autoantibodies (<xref rid="R142" ref-type="bibr">Kim et al. 2016</xref>).</p><p id="P15">Thyroid disease is not the only concern. Clinicians are concerned about subclinically elevated thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH) in early pregnancy because it may be associated with several possible adverse maternal and fetal outcomes (<xref rid="R84" ref-type="bibr">Forhead and Fowden 2014</xref>). This general concern has prompted numerous PFAS-exposure evaluations of corresponding TSH in maternal serum, cord blood, and newborns. A review of maternal and child biomarkers with PFAS exposure noted that higher TSH has been reported in 4 second-trimester studies (<xref rid="R14" ref-type="bibr">Ballesteros et al. 2017</xref>), but there are also conflicting findings. Studies measuring PFAS in the first trimester have also found associations between PFAS exposure and altered TSH levels in newborns, including nonmonotonic patterns of dose response that mirror the marked alterations of thyroid hormone levels during pregnancy (<xref rid="R120" ref-type="bibr">Inoue et al. 2019</xref>).</p><p id="P16">From the available studies, PFAS definitively alter human thyroid hormones and potentially contribute to thyroid auto-immunity but do not so far appear to be a cause of thyroid cancer (<xref rid="R16" ref-type="bibr">Barry et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R281" ref-type="bibr">Vieira et al. 2013</xref>). Also, thyroid cancer is usually survived; thus, morbidity rather than mortality studies are useful.</p></sec><sec id="S5"><title>Liver disease and cancer</title><p id="P17">The liver is a primary target organ for long-chain PFAS storage, and accompanying experimental evidence of toxicity includes hepatocyte fat infiltration, specific P450 (CYP) pathway induction, apoptosis, hepatocellular adenomas and carcinomas, and disrupted fatty acid trafficking that can be peroxisome proliferator-activated receptor alpha (PPARα)-dependent or -independent and present across species (<xref rid="R175" ref-type="bibr">Maestri et al. 2006</xref>; <xref rid="R57" ref-type="bibr">Cui et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R283" ref-type="bibr">Wan et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R113" ref-type="bibr">Huang et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R207" ref-type="bibr">Perez et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R80" ref-type="bibr">Filgo et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R311" ref-type="bibr">Xu et al. 2016</xref>, <xref rid="R312" ref-type="bibr">2020a</xref>; <xref rid="R316" ref-type="bibr">Yao et al. 2016</xref>; Zhang L et al. 2016b; <xref rid="R115" ref-type="bibr">Hui et al. 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R156" ref-type="bibr">Li et al. 2017a</xref>; <xref rid="R103" ref-type="bibr">Guillette et al. 2020</xref>; <xref rid="R186" ref-type="bibr">National Toxicology Program 2020a</xref>).</p><p id="P18">Population studies demonstrate significant associations of long-chain PFAS (&gt;6 fluorinated carbons) exposure to higher liver enzymes, such as alanine aminotransferase in adults and adolescents (<xref rid="R228" ref-type="bibr">Sakr et al. 2007a</xref>; <xref rid="R90" ref-type="bibr">Gallo et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R314" ref-type="bibr">Yamaguchi et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R95" ref-type="bibr">Gleason et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R10" ref-type="bibr">Attanasio 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R194" ref-type="bibr">Nian et al. 2019</xref>), including in longitudinal studies (<xref rid="R229" ref-type="bibr">Sakr et al. 2007b</xref>; <xref rid="R59" ref-type="bibr">Darrow et al. 2016</xref>). Following low-dose exposures, these associations may be more evident in obese participants (<xref rid="R162" ref-type="bibr">Lin et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R90" ref-type="bibr">Gallo et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R127" ref-type="bibr">Jain and Ducatman 2019e</xref>).</p><p id="P19">Based on experimental data (<xref rid="R176" ref-type="bibr">Martin et al. 2007</xref>; <xref rid="R283" ref-type="bibr">Wan et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R285" ref-type="bibr">Wang et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R62" ref-type="bibr">Das et al. 2017</xref>), nonalcoholic fatty liver disease (NAFLD) has been investigated as a clinical outcome of PFAS exposure mediating consistent population PFAS-altered liver enzyme findings. Studies with NAFLD cytokeratin C18 biomarkers have provided supportive evidence for PFAS inducing steatosis (<xref rid="R18" ref-type="bibr">Bassler et al. 2019</xref>). Metabolomic studies have been directed at potentially explanatory human glycerophosphocholine and fatty acid profiles (<xref rid="R143" ref-type="bibr">Kingsley et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R232" ref-type="bibr">Salihovic et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R282" ref-type="bibr">Wahlang et al. 2019</xref>). Processes which favor steatosis promote advanced liver disease including liver cancer in humans (<xref rid="R178" ref-type="bibr">Massoud and Charlton 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R186" ref-type="bibr">National Toxicology Program 2020a</xref>). Associations of PFAS with advanced human liver disease and liver cancer are technically hard to study for reasons including (and not limited to) lethality, selection of comparison populations, and alterations of excretion mechanics associated with disease states. In a clinic-based study, mostly obese (85%) children aged 7 to 19 yr with biopsy-proven NAFLD had more advanced disease associated with PFOS and PFHxS exposure as well as associations with lipid and amino acid pathways linked to NAFLD pathogenesis (<xref rid="R130" ref-type="bibr">Jin et al. 2020</xref>). However, an adult study reported that serum PFHxS was inversely associated with hepatic lobular inflammation in morbidly obese bariatric surgery patients (<xref rid="R218" ref-type="bibr">Rantakokko et al. 2015</xref>). A study of heavily exposed workers (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 462, geometric mean serum PFOA of 4048 ng/mL) detected significantly increased incident mortality for cirrhosis (relative risk = 3.87, 95% CI 1.18–12.7) and liver cancer (relative risk = 6.69, 95% CI 1.71–26.2) compared to a regional population (<xref rid="R94" ref-type="bibr">Girardi and Merler 2019</xref>), whereas no PFAS association to cancer or advanced liver disease was reported in a 3M worker cohort or in the C8 Health study population (<xref rid="R173" ref-type="bibr">Lundin et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R16" ref-type="bibr">Barry et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R281" ref-type="bibr">Vieira et al. 2013</xref>).</p><p id="P20">Emerging animal toxicology and histology and human population data provide mechanistic clues that PFAS disrupt hepatic metabolism, leading to increased bile acid reuptake and lipid accumulation in liver (<xref rid="R231" ref-type="bibr">Salihovic et al. 2020</xref>; <xref rid="R236" ref-type="bibr">Schlezinger et al. 2020</xref>). A review of NAFLD and toxicant exposure concluded that PFAS are associated with early steatosis (“fatty liver”), the preclinical stage of NAFLD (<xref rid="R8" ref-type="bibr">Armstrong and Guo 2019</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="S6"><title>Lipid and insulin dysregulation</title><p id="P21">Cross-sectional and longitudinal investigations indicate that PFAS increase serum total and low-density lipoprotein cholesterol in adults and children (<xref rid="R252" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R85" ref-type="bibr">Frisbee et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R189" ref-type="bibr">Nelson et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R74" ref-type="bibr">Eriksen et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R81" ref-type="bibr">Fisher et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R82" ref-type="bibr">Fitz-Simon et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R92" ref-type="bibr">Geiger et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R88" ref-type="bibr">Fu et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R249" ref-type="bibr">Starling et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R303" ref-type="bibr">Winquist and Steenland 2014a</xref>; <xref rid="R242" ref-type="bibr">Skuladottir et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R320" ref-type="bibr">Zeng et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R147" ref-type="bibr">Koshy et al. 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R54" ref-type="bibr">Convertino et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R109" ref-type="bibr">He et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R237" ref-type="bibr">Seo et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R70" ref-type="bibr">Dong et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R164" ref-type="bibr">Lin et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R157" ref-type="bibr">Li et al. 2020</xref>; <xref rid="R165" ref-type="bibr">Liu G et al. 2020</xref>), including clinically defined high cholesterol (<xref rid="R252" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R303" ref-type="bibr">Winquist and Steenland 2014a</xref>; <xref rid="R164" ref-type="bibr">Lin et al. 2019</xref>). Studies of large populations, featuring wide exposure ranges, demonstrate that serum lipids rapidly increase beginning at background (1–10 ng/mL) serum concentration and then are followed by attenuating (“plateaued”) cholesterol measurements as (log-transformed) exposures to long-chain PFAS increase (<xref rid="R252" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R85" ref-type="bibr">Frisbee et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R157" ref-type="bibr">Li et al. 2020</xref>). These findings suggest partially saturable mechanisms; thus, the cholesterol dose response at pharmacologic or acutely toxic doses should be viewed with caution; associations can be missed or may be misleading when an environmental range of exposure is absent. At background exposure levels, residual associations may be more detectable in obese participants (<xref rid="R270" ref-type="bibr">Timmermann et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R126" ref-type="bibr">Jain and Ducatman 2019d</xref>), a finding congruent with experimental PFAS outcomes in rodents fed “Western” or high-fat diets (<xref rid="R265" ref-type="bibr">Tan et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R216" ref-type="bibr">Quist et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R221" ref-type="bibr">Rebholz et al. 2016</xref>). Human gene expression pathways provide support for an interaction of obesity and PFAS exposures and suggest possible sex differences (<xref rid="R83" ref-type="bibr">Fletcher et al. 2013</xref>). A pharmacokinetic model predicts that approximately half of the PFOS-exposed population would experience a &gt;20% rise in serum cholesterol (<xref rid="R50" ref-type="bibr">Chou and Lin 2020</xref>). Risk-assessment implications for low-PFAS dose increases in cholesterol have been noted (<xref rid="R190" ref-type="bibr">New Jersey Drinking Water Quality Institute Health Effects Subcommittee 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R157" ref-type="bibr">Li et al. 2020</xref>), and a review of population and toxicity data concluded that dyslipidemia is the strongest metabolic outcome of PFAS exposure (<xref rid="R261" ref-type="bibr">Sunderland et al. 2019</xref>).</p><p id="P22">Human PFAS lipid findings may be related to experimental findings of induced adipogenesis, impaired bile acid metabolism/synthesis, strongly decreased CYP7A1 enzyme activity, altered fatty acid transport, and intracellular lipid accumulation with steatosis, including in PPAR-α-null or PPAR-α-humanized animals (<xref rid="R104" ref-type="bibr">Guruge et al. 2006</xref>; <xref rid="R152" ref-type="bibr">Lau et al. 2007</xref>; <xref rid="R24" ref-type="bibr">Bijland et al. 2011</xref>; <xref rid="R26" ref-type="bibr">Bjork et al. 2011</xref>; <xref rid="R286" ref-type="bibr">Wang et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R80" ref-type="bibr">Filgo et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R62" ref-type="bibr">Das et al. 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R232" ref-type="bibr">Salihovic et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R321" ref-type="bibr">Zhang et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R19" ref-type="bibr">Behr et al. 2020a</xref>; Liu S et al. 2020b; <xref rid="R236" ref-type="bibr">Schlezinger et al. 2020</xref>). Independent of PFAS exposure, similar alterations in metabolic pathways have been related to disrupted fatty acid beta-oxidation and increased free cholesterol in toxicology studies (<xref rid="R208" ref-type="bibr">Perla et al. 2017</xref>).</p><p id="P23">Cross-sectional studies of diabetes outcomes can be misleading for reasons discussed in the renal section (see section <xref rid="S7" ref-type="sec">Kidney disease, uric acid, and kidney cancer</xref>). Emerging longitudinal and diabetes clinical trial data indicate that PFAS may increase human insulin resistance, associated with dysregulated lipogenesis activity (<xref rid="R3" ref-type="bibr">Alderete et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R164" ref-type="bibr">Lin et al. 2019</xref>). Longitudinal studies of clinically diagnosed diabetes patients have sometimes associated PFAS exposures with diabetes (<xref rid="R260" ref-type="bibr">Sun et al. 2018</xref>) or with small changes in glycemic markers (<xref rid="R39" ref-type="bibr">Cardenas et al. 2017</xref>); however, diabetes associations to date are not consistent (<xref rid="R136" ref-type="bibr">Karnes et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R39" ref-type="bibr">Cardenas et al. 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R69" ref-type="bibr">Donat-Vargas et al. 2019</xref>). Future studies should consider whether PFAS may instigate autoimmune diabetic outcomes in humans, as shown in experimental studies (<xref rid="R29" ref-type="bibr">Bodin et al. 2016</xref>). Experimental data reveal that PFAS activate G protein-coupled receptor 40, a free fatty acid-regulated membrane receptor on islet ß cells, stimulating insulin secretion (<xref rid="R212" ref-type="bibr">Qin et al. 2020</xref>; <xref rid="R323" ref-type="bibr">Zhang L et al. 2020</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="S7"><title>Kidney disease, uric acid, and kidney cancer</title><p id="P24">Extended human half-lives of long-chain PFAS are attributed to active renal tubular reabsorption. Of concern, legacy PFAS such as PFOA and PFOS are concentrated in renal tissues, and histopathologic, molecular, oxidative stress, and epigenetic studies provide evidence of potential nephrotoxicity (<xref rid="R295" ref-type="bibr">Wen et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R248" ref-type="bibr">Stanifer et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R230" ref-type="bibr">Sakuma et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R220" ref-type="bibr">Rashid et al. 2020</xref>). In addition, the strong influence of kidney reabsorption on the extended half-lives of long-chain PFAS is consistent with both human protein binding and experimental PFAS excretion data.</p><p id="P25">Human studies have associated legacy PFAS exposure to diminished glomerular filtration and/or defined chronic kidney disease in adults and children (<xref rid="R240" ref-type="bibr">Shankar et al. 2011</xref>; <xref rid="R290" ref-type="bibr">Watkins et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R138" ref-type="bibr">Kataria et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R28" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2018</xref>). However, this outcome may be due to reverse causation (<xref rid="R290" ref-type="bibr">Watkins et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R64" ref-type="bibr">Dhingra et al. 2017</xref>). Some reviews of the available epidemiologic and toxicologic evidence suggest causative links between PFAS and diminished kidney function and chronic kidney disease (<xref rid="R248" ref-type="bibr">Stanifer et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R79" ref-type="bibr">Ferrari et al. 2019</xref>); these authors also note several knowledge gaps and uncertainty about which proposed mechanisms of action are most important. A propensity score approach to NHANES data (<xref rid="R125" ref-type="bibr">Jain and Ducatman 2019c</xref>; <xref rid="R327" ref-type="bibr">Zhao et al. 2020</xref>) and a study with repeated PFAS and health measures over an 18-yr period (<xref rid="R28" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2018</xref>) recently concluded that PFAS exposure likely causes diminished renal glomerular filtration.</p><p id="P26">Uric acid, a biomarker of increased risk for renal disease (<xref rid="R195" ref-type="bibr">Obermayr et al. 2008</xref>), is also consistently associated with PFAS exposure in adults and children (<xref rid="R253" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R92" ref-type="bibr">Geiger et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R95" ref-type="bibr">Gleason et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R138" ref-type="bibr">Kataria et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R213" ref-type="bibr">Qin et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R319" ref-type="bibr">Zeng et al. 2019</xref>), including a visible dose-response curve that begins at or near historic background levels in human populations (<xref rid="R253" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R319" ref-type="bibr">Zeng et al. 2019</xref>). Serum PFAS concentrations exhibit an inverted U-shaped pattern related to glomerular filtration, initially exhibiting a modest accumulation as glomerular filtration begins to decrease and then decreasing in advancing renal disease, likely due to failure of normal strong reabsorption mechanisms in moderate to severe kidney disease (<xref rid="R125" ref-type="bibr">Jain and Ducatman 2019c</xref>). This finding is more dramatic across stages of glomerular filtration when there is also albuminuria (<xref rid="R124" ref-type="bibr">Jain and Ducatman 2019b</xref>). Studies suggest that the association of PFAS to uric acid is not due to reverse causation and is underestimated because the failing kidney excretes long-chain PFAS but retains uric acid. An implication is that population outcomes that occur in the presence of either albuminuria or moderate to severe renal disease such as hypertension (<xref rid="R122" ref-type="bibr">Jain 2020</xref>) increasing presence of and uric acid (a biomarker of renal disease; <xref rid="R123" ref-type="bibr">Jain and Ducatman 2019a</xref>; <xref rid="R319" ref-type="bibr">Zeng et al. 2019</xref>) can be underestimated in cross-sectional studies; in other words, the link between these health outcomes and PFAS exposure is obscured in these studies because of enhanced PFAS excretion patterns in the presence of either albuminuria or moderate to severe kidney disease. Furthermore, the strong influence of renal reabsorption on the long half-lives of long chain PFAS is consistent with both human protein binding of PFAS and experimental PFAS excretion rates in high-dose rodent studies (<xref rid="R47" ref-type="bibr">Cheng and Ng 2017</xref>).</p><p id="P27">Kidney cancer diagnoses have been increasing since 1975, a finding that is partially independent of improved detection, with 5-yr cancer-specific survival of approximately 80% (<xref rid="R91" ref-type="bibr">Gandaglia et al. 2014</xref>). The C8 Health studies noted longitudinal (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 32 254) increases of kidney cancer (hazard ratio = 1.10, 95% CI 0.98–1.24) and kidney cancer mortality (<xref rid="R254" ref-type="bibr">Steenland and Woskie 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R16" ref-type="bibr">Barry et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R281" ref-type="bibr">Vieira et al. 2013</xref>). A review of 6 published studies found long-chain PFAS exposure associated with kidney cancer or kidney cancer mortality, with risks ranging from 1.07 to 12.8 (<xref rid="R248" ref-type="bibr">Stanifer et al. 2018</xref>). Subsequent preliminary data from the heavily exposed Veneto, Italy, population also suggest a significant increase in kidney cancer mortality with PFAS exposure (<xref rid="R179" ref-type="bibr">Mastrantonio et al. 2018</xref>). Evidence is accumulating for PFAS as a cause of chronic disease and kidney cancer. Study designs must consider the peculiar PFAS excretion mechanics involved in and associated with kidney disease.</p></sec><sec id="S8"><title>Reproductive and developmental outcomes</title><p id="P28">Exposure to PFOA impairs human sperm motility and sperm penetration into viscous media (<xref rid="R227" ref-type="bibr">Sabovic et al. 2020</xref>; <xref rid="R317" ref-type="bibr">Yuan et al. 2020</xref>) and is longitudinally associated with lower sperm concentration and count and higher adjusted levels of luteinizing and follicle-stimulating hormones in young men (<xref rid="R131" ref-type="bibr">Joensen et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R279" ref-type="bibr">Vested et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R246" ref-type="bibr">Song et al. 2018</xref>). Serum concentrations of PFAS are also cross-sectionally associated with deleterious markers of semen quality (<xref rid="R169" ref-type="bibr">Louis et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R203" ref-type="bibr">Pan et al. 2019</xref>).</p><p id="P29">Legacy and emerging PFAS have been found in follicular fluid (<xref rid="R135" ref-type="bibr">Kang et al. 2020</xref>). They appear to alter endometrial regulation such as progesterone activity in young women (<xref rid="R66" ref-type="bibr">Di Nisio et al. 2020b</xref>) and possibly menstrual cycle length (<xref rid="R172" ref-type="bibr">Lum et al. 2017</xref>). Associations with menarche and menopause may be substantially due to reverse causation because menstruation is a route by which women eliminate PFAS (<xref rid="R64" ref-type="bibr">Dhingra et al. 2017</xref>), partially explaining why men have higher PFAS levels than women in the same communities. Women on birth control and who do not menstruate or with poor cyclicity because of age, activity level, or disease may have elevated PFAS levels in comparison with menstruating women. Exposure to PFAS has been associated with endometriosis in the United States and in China (<xref rid="R170" ref-type="bibr">Louis et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R38" ref-type="bibr">Campbell et al. 2016</xref>; Wang B et al. 2017a), but the specific PFAS associated with this effect vary among studies.</p><p id="P30">Time-to-pregnancy (fecundity) studies provide indirect evidence of changes in fertility. Methodologic considerations include maternal and paternal age, parity (which in turn affects serum PFAS), and health status. Among 1240 women in the Danish National Birth Cohort, PFOS exposure was associated with decreased fecundity (median serum PFOS 35.5 ng/mL; <xref rid="R77" ref-type="bibr">Fei et al. 2009</xref>). Reverse causation may explain this finding because it is duplicated in parous, but not among nonparous, women (<xref rid="R299" ref-type="bibr">Whitworth et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R13" ref-type="bibr">Bach et al. 2015</xref>). Prospective odds of actual infertility in the Maternal-Infant Research on Environmental Chemicals cohort (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 1743) at low-dose exposures were associated with PFOA (geometric mean 1.66 ng/mL; odds ratio = 1.31, 95% CI 1.11–1.53) and PFHxS (odds ratio = 1.27, 95% CI 1.09–1.48; <xref rid="R278" ref-type="bibr">Velez et al. 2015</xref>). The reported fertility rate improved following water filtration in a PFAS-contaminated community (incidence rate ratio 0.73, 95% CI 0.69–0.77 prior to filtration) along with measures of birth weight (<xref rid="R289" ref-type="bibr">Waterfield et al. 2020</xref>).</p><p id="P31">Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances reliably move across the placenta and enter breast milk (<xref rid="R105" ref-type="bibr">Gyllenhammar et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R277" ref-type="bibr">VanNoy et al. 2018</xref>); serum PFAS levels in young children generally exceed maternal serum concentrations (<xref rid="R87" ref-type="bibr">Fromme et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R204" ref-type="bibr">Papadopoulou et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R75" ref-type="bibr">Eryasa et al. 2019</xref>). Population studies provide evidence that breastfeeding duration and milk quantity are adversely affected by PFAS exposure (<xref rid="R223" ref-type="bibr">Romano et al. 2016</xref>; <xref rid="R271" ref-type="bibr">Timmermann et al. 2017b</xref>; <xref rid="R224" ref-type="bibr">Rosen et al. 2018</xref>).</p><p id="P32">A systematic review reported that PFOA exposure was associated with a small decrease in infant birth weight; the meta-analysis estimated that a 1-ng/mL increase in PFOA was associated with an approximately 19-g reduction (95% CI −29.8 to −7.9 g) in birth weight (<xref rid="R151" ref-type="bibr">Lam et al. 2014</xref>). The authors noted similarities in experimental studies (<xref rid="R133" ref-type="bibr">Johnson et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R148" ref-type="bibr">Koustas et al. 2014</xref>) and concluded that there was “sufficient” human and corroborative toxicology evidence of a detrimental effect of PFOA on birth weight (<xref rid="R133" ref-type="bibr">Johnson et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R148" ref-type="bibr">Koustas et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R151" ref-type="bibr">Lam et al. 2014</xref>). However, another meta-subpopulation analysis, focused on early pregnancy or the time shortly before conception, detected only a small and nonsignificant association, which was less subject to bias (<xref rid="R250" ref-type="bibr">Steenland et al. 2018a</xref>). Different approaches to the possible confounding role of shifting glomerular filtration rates in pregnancy can affect interpretations; evidence suggests this consideration can, at most, only partially explain associations of PFAS exposure to decreased birth weight (<xref rid="R121" ref-type="bibr">Interstate Technology and Regulatory Council 2020</xref>; <xref rid="R300" ref-type="bibr">Wikstrom et al. 2020</xref>). A recent review of mostly prospective cohort studies (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 24 studies) noted PFAS associated with altered fetal and postnatal growth measures, such as lower birth weight. Many (<italic toggle="yes">n</italic> = 22) of the relevant studies suggest developmental and childhood immunomodulatory effects, whereas 21 studies concerning neurodevelopment were inconclusive (<xref rid="R161" ref-type="bibr">Liew et al. 2018</xref>). The authors of the review noted methodologic challenges of developmental and newborn epidemiology, including consideration of critical exposure windows for developmental effects, the effects of breastfeeding and parity on maternal PFAS levels, and the variety of possible mechanistic explanations for growth outcomes, such as disruption of glucocorticoid and thyroid hormone metabolism in utero (<xref rid="R161" ref-type="bibr">Liew et al. 2018</xref>). Recent Faroe Island studies report that prenatal PFAS effects on thyroid hormone status do not support a causal relationship (<xref rid="R309" ref-type="bibr">Xiao et al. 2020</xref>).</p><p id="P33">Review articles suggest that prenatal exposure to PFOA may increase risk of subsequent childhood adiposity, noting that steroid hormones, retinoid X receptor, and other pathways may be contributing to this effect (<xref rid="R107" ref-type="bibr">Halldorsson et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R106" ref-type="bibr">Hall and Greco 2019</xref>). Prospective evidence supports this relationship in adults with a high risk of diabetes (<xref rid="R39" ref-type="bibr">Cardenas et al. 2017</xref>). However, some well-performed community studies do not support this outcome in adults or children (<xref rid="R15" ref-type="bibr">Barry et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R177" ref-type="bibr">Martinsson et al. 2020</xref>).</p><p id="P34">Based on several preliminary findings, supported by longitudinal follow-up studies (<xref rid="R258" ref-type="bibr">Stein et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R235" ref-type="bibr">Savitz et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R60" ref-type="bibr">Darrow et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R11" ref-type="bibr">Avanasi et al. 2016a</xref>, <xref rid="R12" ref-type="bibr">2016b</xref>), the C8 Science Panel concluded that PFOA is probably linked to pregnancy-induced hypertension or preeclampsia. Population-level evidence implicating additional PFAS having this effect has included studies with longitudinal designs (<xref rid="R114" ref-type="bibr">Huang et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R301" ref-type="bibr">Wikstrom et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R30" ref-type="bibr">Borghese et al. 2020</xref>). Experimental support includes PFAS effects on human trophoblast migration in vitro (<xref rid="R263" ref-type="bibr">Szilagyi et al. 2020</xref>) and recent evidence of PFOA and GenX (or hexafluoropropylene oxide dimer acid) effects on mouse placenta, as well as excessive gestational weight gain (<xref rid="R27" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2020</xref>). However, a recent longitudinal study did not find an association of PFAS with pregnancy-associated hypertension (<xref rid="R116" ref-type="bibr">Huo et al. 2020</xref>).</p><p id="P35">The possibility that circulating PFAS may reduce bone mineral density has been investigated. Cross-sectional and practical trial associations have been found in adults (<xref rid="R163" ref-type="bibr">Lin et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R112" ref-type="bibr">Hu et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R65" ref-type="bibr">Di Nisio et al. 2020a</xref>), and there is emerging longitudinal evidence from a mother and child pair study indicating that children may also be affected (<xref rid="R51" ref-type="bibr">Cluett et al. 2019</xref>).</p><p id="P36">Testicular cancer diagnoses are increasing steadily, a trend unrelated to improved detection (<xref rid="R46" ref-type="bibr">Cheng et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R205" ref-type="bibr">Park et al. 2018</xref>). Most patients diagnosed (&gt;90%) will be cured and die of other causes; mortality studies therefore provide little help in understanding disease risk factors. The C8 Science Panel detected longitudinal evidence for increased testicular cancer risk (1.35, 95% CI 1.00–1.79) for cumulative PFOA exposure (<xref rid="R16" ref-type="bibr">Barry et al. 2013</xref>). There are ample supportive data of testicular damage following PFAS exposure, including strong evidence of endocrine disruption; but the cell-specific associations are different in humans (germ cell) than the outcomes in rodents (stromal).</p><p id="P37">Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances have deleterious effects on conception, pregnancy, and infant development. The underlying birth weight data are mostly supportive, although the subsequent growth and adiposity literature is mixed. The most sensitive reproductive and developmental outcomes are a topic of ongoing discussion.</p><p id="P38">Outcomes replicated across populations, such as perfluorocarboxylate (PFCA) and perfluorosulfonate (PFSA) exposures associated with down-regulation of immune response; increases in cholesterol, liver enzymes, and uric acid; alterations in thyroid hormone binding proteins; growth deficits; and effects on breast milk and lactation, indicate priority areas for understanding mechanisms and health implications.</p></sec></sec><sec id="S9"><title>CURRENT KNOWLEDGE OF PFAS TOXICITY IN EXPERIMENTAL MODELS</title><p id="P39">Animal studies have focused most intensely on PFOA and PFOS, using laboratory rodents and, more recently, zebrafish as models. Perfluoroalkyl acids of varied carbon-chain lengths as well as a few replacement chemicals with ether linkages in the carbon backbone (such as GenX and 3H-perfluoro-3-[(3-methoxy-propoxy)propanoic acid], or ADONA) have also been examined, with outcome profiles thus far generally consistent with legacy chemicals. The varying extent of responses is likely related to toxicokinetic disposition (excretion or half-life) and relative potency and affinity of the individual chemical for binding to receptor proteins. Some PFAS (i.e., PFHxS, PFOA, and PFNA) have longer half-lives in mice than rats and typically much longer half-lives in humans (<xref rid="T1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref>). These differences in elimination kinetics complicate the cross-species evaluation of toxicity. In addition, some PFAS (such as PFOA and PFNA) exhibit a profound sex difference in the rate of chemical elimination and bioaccumulation in the rat: females eliminate them much faster than males (<xref rid="T1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref>). Sex differences in half-lives, although important, are much smaller in humans and have a different explanation. The mouse also typically has more limited sex-based PFAS elimination differences, making this species more amenable for extrapolation to humans, especially for mechanistic and toxicity evaluations.</p><p id="P40">In general, human health effects associated with PFOA and PFOS exposure (described in section <xref rid="S2" ref-type="sec">Current Knowledge of PFAS Toxicity in Humans</xref>) have also been reported in animal models: hepatic/lipid metabolic toxicity, developmental toxicity, immune suppression, tumor induction, endocrine disruption, and obesity. These findings are often derived from well-controlled laboratory experiments in more than one species using wide dose ranges that are often orders of magnitude higher than typical human exposure, to account for differences in half-life across species. Some of the phenotypic findings are supported by in vitro mechanistic investigation and/or molecular queries on target tissues. Our understanding of the toxicologic properties of PFAS other than PFOA and PFOS is notably less advanced and, in the case of emerging replacements and by-products, completely unexplored.</p><sec id="S10"><title>Hepatic and metabolic toxicity</title><p id="P41">In rodent studies, dose-dependent increases in liver weight, in hepatocellular hypertrophy associated with vacuole formation, and with or without increased peroxisome proliferation have been observed with a significant body burden of PFAS, especially for the most persistent and potent long-chain homologs. Hepatocyte proliferation, necrosis, and apoptosis are outcomes occurring at relatively low doses. This is also true for a new replacement chemical, GenX, which altered liver histopathology and function and increased apoptosis in mice and fish (<xref rid="R27" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2020</xref>; <xref rid="R103" ref-type="bibr">Guillette et al. 2020</xref>). Correspondingly, transcriptional activation of mouse and, to a lesser extent, human PPARα-related genes in liver was detected in adult-exposed models; activation of other nuclear receptors such as PPARγ, constitutive androstane receptor (CAR), and pregnane X-receptor (PXR) has also been reported. These nuclear receptors, metabolic sensors that regulate lipid and glucose metabolism and transport and inflammation, tend to be more responsive in tissues of rodents than in humans (<xref rid="R306" ref-type="bibr">Wolf et al. 2012</xref>; <xref rid="R225" ref-type="bibr">Rosen et al. 2017</xref>). Recent work using developmental models reports that mitochondrial dysfunction is associated with hepatocellular hypertrophy in young adult mice (<xref rid="R216" ref-type="bibr">Quist et al., 2015</xref>) and that other fatty acid metabolism pathways are activated (<xref rid="R134" ref-type="bibr">Jones et al. 2003</xref>; <xref rid="R239" ref-type="bibr">Shabalina et al. 2016</xref>). Steatosis is also a common feature of PFAS chronic exposure in rodents. Exposure in rodent models typically decreases serum cholesterol, whereas elevations of circulating cholesterol levels have been reported in humans. The mode of action concerning serum cholesterol is debatable. For example, PFOA exposure increased liver weight, increased liver enzymes, and led to persistent histopathological changes (particularly damage to the bile duct) in livers of wild-type and PPARα-null rodent strains (reviewed in <xref rid="R67" ref-type="bibr">Division of Science and Research, New Jersey Department of Environmental Protection 2019</xref>). Many of these effects are reversible on cessation of PFAS exposure, and this observation has been interpreted by some as evidence of “adaptive” responses to exposure. However, this reversibility is irrelevant to ongoing environmental PFAS exposure (for instance, from drinking water) because exposure will persist until contamination is remediated. In summary, there is a strong confluence of animal toxicology and histology and human population data that PFAS disrupt hepatic metabolism and lead to lipid accumulation in liver, although the mechanism(s) is unclear. Effects on bile acid metabolism, mitochondrial perturbation, and cholestatic mechanisms deserve further investigation at human-relevant exposures.</p></sec><sec id="S11"><title>Reproductive and developmental toxicity</title><p id="P42">Only a few reproductive toxicity studies of males and females are available, primarily focusing on long-chain PFAS. Profound developmental toxicity has been described following gestational and lactational exposure to PFOS, PFOA, and PFNA in mice (<xref rid="R267" ref-type="bibr">Thibodeaux et al. 2003</xref>; <xref rid="R153" ref-type="bibr">Lau et al. 2006</xref>; <xref rid="R61" ref-type="bibr">Das et al. 2015</xref>) and in mice and rats gestationally exposed to GenX (<xref rid="R53" ref-type="bibr">Conley et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R27" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2020</xref>). Neonatal morbidity and mortality were seen with exposure to high doses of legacy PFAS; growth deficits and developmental delays were noted in offspring exposed to lower doses. Evidence of lactation impairment was seen in mice at doses of 5 mg PFOA/kg body weight (<xref rid="R296" ref-type="bibr">White et al. 2007</xref>), leading to increased offspring mortality (<xref rid="R153" ref-type="bibr">Lau et al. 2006</xref>); recent studies have indicated a role of placental dysfunction in these adverse developmental outcomes (<xref rid="R27" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2020</xref>). Deficits of mammary gland development were also observed in mice exposed to PFOA (doses of 1 mg/kg body wt and lower) during gestation, which persisted into adulthood, although these exposure levels did not alter body weight, lactational function, or neonatal growth of offspring (F1 or F2 mice; <xref rid="R174" ref-type="bibr">Macon et al. 2011</xref>; <xref rid="R298" ref-type="bibr">White et al. 2011b</xref>; <xref rid="R272" ref-type="bibr">Tucker et al. 2015</xref>). Systematic reviews support a relationship between in utero exposure to PFOA and PFOS and reduced fetal growth in animals and humans, and the relationship between PFOA and reduced fetal growth in mice was recently validated (<xref rid="R148" ref-type="bibr">Koustas et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R27" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2020</xref>). Also, PFAS are reported to have reproductive effects such as ovulation failure in mice (<xref rid="R326" ref-type="bibr">Zhang Y et al. 2020</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="S12"><title>Immunotoxicity</title><p id="P43">A few long-chain PFAS (PFOS, PFOA, PFNA, and PFDA) have been shown to alter immune status in rodents and non-human primates. Effects are predominantly immunosuppressive and include reductions in thymus and spleen weights and associated immune cell populations, in numbers of circulating immune cells, in certain aspects of innate immunity (i.e., natural killer cell cytotoxicity), in infectious disease resistance, and in antibodies produced in response to an antigen (i.e., analogous to the vaccine response in humans). In their 2018 draft <italic toggle="yes">Toxicological Profile for Perfluoroalkyls</italic>, the US Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry (ATSDR) noted changes to the aforementioned immune parameters observed in experimental rodents exposed to PFOA, PFOS, PFNA, PFHxS, PFDA, perfluorobutanesulfonic acid (PFBS), or perfluorobutanoic acid (PFBA; <xref rid="R2" ref-type="bibr">Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry 2018</xref>). The US National Toxicology Program conducted a systematic review of the immunotoxicological literature for PFOA and PFOS and concluded that PFOA and PFOS were presumed to be immune hazards to humans based on a high level of evidence for suppression of antibody responses in experimental animals and a moderate level of evidence for suppression of antibody responses in humans (<xref rid="R185" ref-type="bibr">National Toxicology Program 2016</xref>). The ATSDR (<xref rid="R2" ref-type="bibr">Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry 2018</xref>) also included a decreased antibody response to vaccines (PFOA, PFOS, PFHxS, and PFDA) and increased risk of asthma diagnosis (PFOA) among the list of adverse health effects in PFAS-exposed humans. Reduction in the antibody response to a vaccine, an adaptive immune function, is a well-accepted measure of immunotoxicity, is consistent with the mode of action for the effects of fatty acids on immune system function (<xref rid="R86" ref-type="bibr">Fritsche 2006</xref>), and is compelling evidence that the immune system is a sensitive target of PFAS.</p></sec><sec id="S13"><title>Tumor induction</title><p id="P44">Per- and polyfluoroalkyl substances are not known to be directly mutagenic; PFOA, PFOS, and other tested PFAS show little or no evidence for induction of gene mutation, clastogenicity, or aneuploidy in vitro or in vivo by a direct mode of action (see <xref rid="R71" ref-type="bibr">EFSA Panel on Contaminants in the Food Chain [2020]</xref> for details). There is evidence that PFAS can induce DNA damage, such as strand breaks, and other genotoxic effects, secondary to oxidative stress (<xref rid="R71" ref-type="bibr">EFSA Panel on Contaminants in the Food Chain 2020</xref>). This occurs at concentrations or doses that are high relative to human environmental exposures to PFAS, and the mechanism is such that their dose-response will be sublinear. Hence, PFAS are unlikely to be of mutagenic concern in exposed populations.</p><p id="P45">In adult-exposed rodents and fish, PFOA and PFOS have been shown to induce tumors. Liver adenomas, pancreatic acinar cell tumors, and testicular Leydig cell adenomas have been detected in rats treated chronically with PFOA (<xref rid="R117" ref-type="bibr">IARC Working Group on the Evaluation of Carcinogenic Risks to Humans 2017</xref>) as well as its replacement, GenX (<xref rid="R42" ref-type="bibr">Caverly Rae et al. 2015</xref>). Following gestational and chronic exposure to PFOA, 58% of male rats demonstrated pancreatic tumors at the lowest dose administered (<xref rid="R187" ref-type="bibr">National Toxicology Program 2020b</xref>). This finding has spurred Minnesota and California policymakers to consider cancer as an endpoint in risk assessment, whereas the European Food Safety Authority (<xref rid="R71" ref-type="bibr">EFSA Panel on Contaminants in the Food Chain 2020</xref>) has the opinion that there is not adequate evidence for a link between exposure to PFAS and cancer risk in humans. This “tumor triad” profile has been associated with the PPARα-mediated molecular signaling pathway in rats exposed to high doses of PFAS. Consequently, liver tumors involving this mode of action are not considered relevant to humans at equivalent PFAS exposures (<xref rid="R210" ref-type="bibr">Post et al. 2017</xref>). The human relevance of PPARα-mediated pancreatic tumors in rodents remains to be determined. Liver lesions evident in PPARα-null mice exposed to PFOA during pregnancy and lactation (<xref rid="R80" ref-type="bibr">Filgo et al. 2015</xref>) suggest a non-PPARα-mediated liver response. Induction of liver tumors mediated by estrogen receptor (ER) activation has also been reported in fish (<xref rid="R268" ref-type="bibr">Tilton et al. 2008</xref>), and several non-PPARα-mediated hypotheses, including increased reactive oxygen species formation, oxidative stress, and mitochondrial dysfunction; decreased tumor cell surveillance by the immune system; and diminished gap junction cellular communication, are documented (<xref rid="R117" ref-type="bibr">IARC Working Group on the Evaluation of Carcinogenic Risks to Humans 2017</xref>; <xref rid="R190" ref-type="bibr">New Jersey Drinking Water Quality Institute Health Effects Subcommittee 2017</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="S14"><title>Endocrine disruption</title><p id="P46">The primary evidence for the endocrine-disrupting potential of PFAS involves induction of hypothyroxinemia and reduction of serum testosterone in rats. An early review of PFAS endocrine-disrupting properties in humans concluded that the “thyroid may be one axis significantly affected by PFOA exposure while the animal toxicology literature is less certain due to technical issues” (<xref rid="R297" ref-type="bibr">White et al. 2011a</xref>).</p><p id="P47">The effects of PFAS on thyroid hormone status detected in animal studies differ from classical hypothyroidism, in that reduction of circulating total thyroxine is not accompanied by a compensatory increase of TSH. A possible mechanism for these effects may be related to the propensity of protein binding of legacy PFAS, which could lead to displaced total thyroxine binding to its carrier proteins (transthyretin and thyroxine-binding globulin). Human population studies augment animal data showing that PFAS interact with thyroid hormone binding proteins (<xref rid="R23" ref-type="bibr">Berg et al. 2015</xref>; <xref rid="R222" ref-type="bibr">Ren et al. 2016</xref>; Zhang J et al. 2016a), one of several mechanisms by which PFAS can perturb feedback relationships between free thyroid hormone available to cells (free total thyroxine) and the hypothalamic-pituitary axis. Some estrogenic effects of PFAS have also been illustrated by in vitro studies, although there is no evidence of direct transactivation of estrogen, androgen, or glucocorticoid receptors (<xref rid="R20" ref-type="bibr">Behr et al. 2018</xref>, <xref rid="R21" ref-type="bibr">2020b</xref>).</p><p id="P48">The evidence for PFAS affecting ER signaling in humans and animals is mixed. Although studies have identified some PFAS as being without estrogenic activity (<xref rid="R20" ref-type="bibr">Behr et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R31" ref-type="bibr">Borghoff et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R96" ref-type="bibr">Gogola et al. 2019</xref>), others suggest an ability of PFAS to modulate or even activate ER-mediated effects (<xref rid="R22" ref-type="bibr">Benninghoff et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R144" ref-type="bibr">Kjeldsen and Bonefeld-Jørgensen 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R287" ref-type="bibr">Wang et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R25" ref-type="bibr">Bjerregaard-Olesen et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R215" ref-type="bibr">Qiu et al. 2020</xref>), with some effects only observed in aquatic organisms (<xref rid="R294" ref-type="bibr">Wei et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R44" ref-type="bibr">Chen et al. 2016</xref>, <xref rid="R45" ref-type="bibr">2018</xref>). Microarray analyses of human primary hepatocytes confirmed that PFOA activated the ER pathway (<xref rid="R34" ref-type="bibr">Buhrke et al. 2015</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="S15"><title>Neurotoxicity</title><p id="P49">Potential adverse effects of PFAS on the nervous system and functions have not been widely investigated. A few studies reported neurotoxicity of PFOS, PFHxS, and PFOA in cell culture systems (<xref rid="R243" ref-type="bibr">Slotkin et al. 2008</xref>), as well as altered behavioral responses (<xref rid="R98" ref-type="bibr">Goulding et al. 2017</xref>) and deficits in learning and memory ability in rodents (<xref rid="R280" ref-type="bibr">Viberg et al. 2013</xref>). In contrast, no significant developmental neurotoxic effects were seen from prenatal exposure to PFOS in USEPA guideline-based studies with rats (<xref rid="R36" ref-type="bibr">Butenhoff et al. 2009</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="S16"><title>Obesity</title><p id="P50">Numerous cell-based assays in human and mouse pre-adipocytes and animal studies with and without high-fat diets have consistently shown that some PFAS have the potential to increase lipid production by adipocytes and fat pads (<xref rid="R276" ref-type="bibr">van Esterik et al. 2016</xref>). Exposure of pregnant mice to low doses of PFOA produced obesity in young adult female offspring (<xref rid="R110" ref-type="bibr">Hines et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R276" ref-type="bibr">van Esterik et al. 2016</xref>), a finding that was re-capitulated in Danish women exposed in utero to PFOA (<xref rid="R107" ref-type="bibr">Halldorsson et al. 2012</xref>). Both PFOA and GenX increased weight gain of pregnant mice (<xref rid="R27" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2020</xref>), an effect also seen in women during pregnancy (<xref rid="R9" ref-type="bibr">Ashley-Martin et al. 2016</xref>), although discordant results have been reported in other studies (<xref rid="R15" ref-type="bibr">Barry et al. 2014</xref>; <xref rid="R193" ref-type="bibr">Ngo et al. 2014</xref>). These apparently disparate findings in experimental models may be associated with differences among mouse strains examined, exposure periods, statistical methodology, and/or the rodent diets used.</p><p id="P51">There are specific differences in human and rodent health outcomes that deserve further investigation: 1) cholesterol metabolism, 2) thyroid effects, 3) mode of action for liver effects (different or same), and 4) kidney transporter or other mode of action leading to large differences in half-life. However, species concordance in the 6 human health effects discussed in the present review supports a weight of evidence for these effect for the handful of extensively studied PFAS.</p><p id="P52">Human health advisory and guidance values for a few PFAS have been issued to date by the USEPA, the ATSDR, several individual state environmental agencies or health departments, as well as regulatory agencies in Canada and Europe that are largely (but not exclusively) based on toxicological findings in animal models. However, risk-assessment scientists have not reached consensus in selecting a singular apical endpoint as the basis for a point of departure for assessments. Three toxicological features of PFAS that have been commonly highlighted, based on their sensitivity (low dose effect), strength of evidence (robust corroborating studies with mechanistic support for human relevance), and corresponding findings noted in epidemiological investigation, are hepatotoxicity (and alterations in lipid metabolism), developmental toxicity, and immunotoxicity. It should be noted that apical endpoints that drive risk assessments often differ among individual PFAS, perhaps highlighting the complexity of these chemicals and the family of PFAS, in general.</p></sec></sec><sec id="S17"><title>IMPORTANCE OF TOXICOKINETICS IN UNDERSTANDING PFAS TOXICITY</title><sec id="S18"><title>Species and sex differences</title><p id="P53">Few of the substantial number of structurally diverse PFAS have been tested for toxicological effects. Some available toxicological information has come from studies in animals, where marked species and (in rat) sex differences in half-life for some PFAS (<xref rid="T1" ref-type="table">Table 1</xref>) have been observed and the relevance to humans is uncertain. These differences are due to toxicokinetic and toxicodynamic factors. There are also differences in mean PFAS serum levels between men and women in the same communities. Children may have elevated serum levels compared to parents, even with the same exposures (<xref rid="R73" ref-type="bibr">Emmett et al. 2006</xref>; <xref rid="R58" ref-type="bibr">Daly et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R99" ref-type="bibr">Graber et al. 2019</xref>), for reasons relating to transplacental transfer, breastfeeding, and body mass (<xref rid="R73" ref-type="bibr">Emmett et al. 2006</xref>; <xref rid="R58" ref-type="bibr">Daly et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R99" ref-type="bibr">Graber et al. 2019</xref>; <xref rid="R27" ref-type="bibr">Blake et al. 2020</xref>). Transplacental transfer of PFAS confers a substantial burden to the newborn infant. Because the infant has a smaller overall mass and blood volume, PFAS are concentrated, increasing PFAS per volume (<xref rid="R146" ref-type="bibr">Koponen et al. 2018</xref>). In addition, transfer of PFAS is common through lactation, and the longer a child breastfeeds, the higher the body burden (<xref rid="R105" ref-type="bibr">Gyllenhammar et al. 2018</xref>; <xref rid="R277" ref-type="bibr">VanNoy et al. 2018</xref>).</p></sec><sec id="S19"><title>Effects of comorbidity on PFAS toxicokinetics</title><p id="P54">Factors affecting renal function can influence PFAS toxicokinetics. As discussed, opposing types of causation should be considered. Human toxicokinetics appear to vary bidirectionally with changing renal function, leading to nonmonotonic dose-response relationships and, depending on the study goal, possibly to errors in estimating disease associations. As progress is made in the field of PFAS toxicokinetics, new chemistries may have different clearance factors and nuances that vary by PFAS group or structures, and that will need to be investigated to accurately model half-lives in different exposure subgroups.</p></sec><sec id="S20"><title>Sources of information on toxicokinetics in humans: strengths and limitations of studies</title><p id="P55">Some PFAS half-life data in humans were obtained from retired industry workers, particularly those who worked with PFOS, PFOA, and PFHxS (<xref rid="R196" ref-type="bibr">Olsen et al. 2007</xref>). Since then, these estimates have been modified slightly or confirmed with longitudinal data and modeling from contaminated communities once uncontaminated water options were provided (<xref rid="R17" ref-type="bibr">Bartell et al. 2010</xref>; <xref rid="R159" ref-type="bibr">Li et al. 2018</xref>). Other contemporary PFAS estimates are derived from biomonitoring studies of production workers, blood donors, study participants, and/or occupationally exposed cohorts (<xref rid="R197" ref-type="bibr">Olsen et al. 2009</xref>, <xref rid="R198" ref-type="bibr">2017</xref>; <xref rid="R226" ref-type="bibr">Russell et al. 2013</xref>; <xref rid="R325" ref-type="bibr">Zhang et al. 2013</xref>). Some caution must be taken in using these data because variables affecting PFAS clearance may not be taken into consideration (age, sex, menstruation, disease, and medication status) and may contribute to confounding.</p><p id="P56">The challenge in determining a reliable human half-life in these types of studies is that exposure does not end with a clean water source, retirement, or a change of job and that continued exposures vary over potential depuration periods. Model components may also vary in subclasses. Children (small blood volumes and a large fraction of exposures comes from drinking), pregnant women (large increase in blood volume and water intake), parous women (transfer to fetus and breast milk), and athletes (water intake elevated) are examples of subpopulations with expected variation in half-life compared to adult men (<xref rid="R210" ref-type="bibr">Post et al. 2017</xref>). There will be more human estimates of PFAS forthcoming that involve variations in half-life (<xref rid="R210" ref-type="bibr">Post et al. 2017</xref>). Realistic computational modeling can help, so long as it clearly characterizes exposures and applicable populations. The continued goal should be to provide predictive values for those PFAS lacking actual measurements, based on chemical structures and trusted physiological parameters.</p></sec><sec id="S21"><title>Physiologically based pharmacokinetic/toxicokinetic modeling in different-aged populations</title><p id="P57">In the blood and other tissues, PFAS toxicokinetics are influenced by their interactions with proteins (<xref rid="R5" ref-type="bibr">Andersen et al. 2006</xref>; <xref rid="R137" ref-type="bibr">Katakura et al. 2007</xref>; <xref rid="R184" ref-type="bibr">Nakagawa et al. 2008</xref>; <xref rid="R291" ref-type="bibr">Weaver et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="F2" ref-type="fig">Figure 2</xref>). Certain toxicokinetic features are saturable, and thus dosing in toxicokinetic studies is of profound importance. Studies of renal reabsorption mechanisms in mammals show that reduced activity of transporters such as organic anion transporting polypeptide 1a1, through inactivation (e.g., genetic manipulation, castration, treatment with estrogen) or by saturation at increasing doses, leads to substantial reductions in half-lives of PFOA and PFOS (<xref rid="R5" ref-type="bibr">Andersen et al. 2006</xref>; <xref rid="R184" ref-type="bibr">Nakagawa et al. 2008</xref>; <xref rid="R291" ref-type="bibr">Weaver et al. 2009</xref>; <xref rid="R315" ref-type="bibr">Yang et al. 2009</xref>).</p><p id="P58">These protein-associated toxicokinetic processes were recently incorporated into a model for PFOA in the male Sprague-Dawley rat (<xref rid="R47" ref-type="bibr">Cheng and Ng 2017</xref>), which provides a useful platform to explore how changes in protein interactions might affect estimates of PFAS half-life (<xref rid="F3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3</xref>). At high doses, it is typical to see clear biphasic behavior with rapid initial clearance, during which the serum half-life appears to be shorter especially at high enough doses that processes such as renal reabsorption are saturated, followed by a much longer tail (<xref rid="F3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3A</xref>). In a similar fashion, the magnitude of internal dose and rate of serum clearance can be profoundly influenced by proteins known to bind PFAS, such as serum albumin (<xref rid="F3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3B</xref>). Increasing and decreasing the extent of reabsorption in the kidney increases and decreases the serum half-life, respectively (<xref rid="F3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3C</xref>). Finally, the effect of saturating reabsorption is magnified when the half-life is longer because of increased serum binding (<xref rid="F3" ref-type="fig">Figure 3D</xref>). In this case, taking an initial slope to calculate the serum half-life at high doses would lead to a profound underestimation.</p><p id="P59">Differences in protein expression, circulating levels, and even protein type across populations, sex, and species could lead to important species and sex differences in PFAS biological half-lives (<xref rid="R108" ref-type="bibr">Han et al. 2012</xref>); such differences should be investigated and taken into account in the extrapolation to human equivalent doses. Because expression of proteins may change at different life stages, clearance factors and toxicokinetics may also change.</p><p id="P60">Given the large number of species-, sex-, and age-specific differences that have been observed, coupled with the lack of data for many PFAS, the parameterization of complex physiologically based toxicokinetic models remains a persistent challenge. Therefore, lower-resolution models (e.g., one-compartment or few-compartment models) may be more appropriate for species and settings where insufficient data are available for reasonably accurate parameterization.
